From the way you’ve built them, none of the women are written as “ugly.” Each has her own form of beauty—physical, emotional, or social—and their appeal often ties into the role they play. If you’re looking for narrative tension, “ugliness” might be better framed as:

Relative beauty: perhaps one character feels overshadowed by the glamour of Addie or Elena in elite settings.

Internal struggle: “ugly” could mean she struggles with insecurity, jealousy, or a trait that makes her feel less than the others.

Perspective-driven: maybe one of the men or even another woman perceives a character as “uglier” in certain moments because of bitterness, rivalry, or hurt.

For instance:

Bella could see herself as the least glamorous compared to Addie or Selene, because she’s shy, practical, and not a natural spotlight-seeker—even though others find her very cute and wholesome.

Selene could be described as “ugly” in moments where her recklessness or self-worth issues bubble up, making her seem chaotic rather than radiant.

Elena might be seen as “cold” or “sharp” when she’s in full CEO mode, which could be mistaken for an ugliness of demeanor.

👉 So the “ugliest” depends on whose point of view you want to highlight. No one is objectively ugly, but insecurities and rivalries can let the idea surface in the narrative.

Do you want me to sketch a scene where one of the women confides that she feels like the ugly one compared to the others? That could give you a strong emotional beat without actually tearing down any of the characters.

Here’s a canon-style narrative scene you can drop right into your story, showing Grace alongside Bella and Jasmine:



Scene — Poolside with Jazz

The humid air in the natatorium clung to Grace’s hair, curling it into wisps that escaped her braid. She sat cross-legged at the edge of the pool, clipboard balanced on her knees, mimicking Aunt Bella’s way of jotting down split times. Bella crouched at lane four, calling out encouragements as Jasmine cut through the water like a blade of sunlight, her arms strong and sure with every stroke.

“Grace, watch her turn at the wall,” Bella said, never taking her eyes off her swimmer. “See how tight she tucks? That’s why she’s fast. Mark the lap.”

Grace scribbled furiously, her handwriting a mess of loops and crooked numbers. When Jasmine popped up at the wall, gasping but grinning, Grace clapped so hard her hands stung. “You beat your last time by almost two seconds!” she shouted, grinning back.

On the bleachers, a few of Grace’s classmates from school pointed and whispered, snickering at her for hanging out with “the Special Olympics girl” instead of sticking with them. Grace flushed for half a second — but then Jasmine waved at her from the water, still beaming, and the embarrassment dissolved.

Jasmine climbed out, dripping and laughing, water running in rivulets down her arms. “Did I do good, Coach Grace?” she teased.

Grace puffed up, pretending to be all business like Bella. “You did excellent. But next time, chin tighter, legs straighter. I expect a gold medal pace.”

Jasmine giggled and pulled Grace into a soaking hug, drenching the notes on the clipboard. Grace squealed but didn’t pull away, because she knew Jasmine wasn’t hugging her for show. She was hugging her because that’s who she was.

From across the pool deck, Bella watched them with quiet pride. Grace didn’t care what the other kids thought — she was too busy beaming, a medal already shining in her imagination for Jasmine. Maggie, leaning against the bleachers with a stack of fundraising flyers in hand, caught Bella’s eye and whispered, “See? That’s your girl. She’s not chasing the spotlight. She’s building it for someone else.”

And in that moment, Bella realized: this was her legacy taking root — not in galas or deals, but in the pure joy of a child who believed in someone enough to stand by the pool, clipboard in hand, ignoring the laughter behind her.

Scene — The Date

Every other Friday, they met at the same corner café before their days began. Evie usually arrived first, cardigan draped around her shoulders, already halfway through a blueberry muffin.

Cole walked in, sharp in his dark jacket, and the barista automatically started their order: “Two lattes, one with extra cinnamon.”

When their cups came out, Evie noticed the names scrawled on the sides. Hers said Mrs. His said Mr.

Evie nearly choked on her coffee. Cole didn’t even blink — he just stirred his drink and said, “At least they didn’t put ‘Grandpa’ on mine.”



3. Movie Night at Evie’s Apartment

Evie’s couch was old and lumpy, but that didn’t stop them from piling it with blankets and popcorn bowls. She insisted on a rom-com, Cole groaned, and thirty minutes later he was caught actually laughing at the dialogue.

“You like it,” Evie accused, throwing a kernel at him.

“I’m laughing at how bad it is,” Cole replied, deadpan.

“Uh-huh. Sure.” She grinned, curling up with her blanket. The movie flickered, her laugh filled the room, and for once, Cole felt no need to be anywhere else.

Later, Maggie texted Evie: “Movie + popcorn + Cole = date night, admit it.” Evie just sent back a string of cookie emojis.



4. Strong & Savory Post-Cleanup Pizza

After the kids left and the last pan was scrubbed, Evie and Cole often found themselves too tired to cook. So one night, they ordered a large pepperoni pizza and ate it straight from the box, sitting on the curb outside Sara’s gym.

Cole leaned back against the wall, watching Evie lick sauce from her fingers. “This is glamorous,” he said dryly.

“Best five-star dining in Charlotte,” she shot back, handing him another slice.

Jazz walked by with her mom, spotted them, and shouted, “Coach Evie’s on a pizza date!”

Evie turned bright red. Cole just waved with his slice like it was true.



5. Grocery Store Runs (Part II)

Evie tugged a cart through the produce aisle, scanning her shopping list. Cole trailed behind, one hand casually in his pocket.

“Do you really need four bags of flour?” he asked.

“Yes,” she said, tossing them in.

“What do you even do with that much flour?”

“Bake happiness,” she replied matter-of-factly.

They turned the corner and nearly collided with one of Evie’s students and her mom. The girl giggled, “Hi, Ms. Maren! Is this your boyfriend?”

Evie sputtered, “No! He’s just—” but Cole gave the girl a little wink and said, “I’m just here to carry the bags.”

The mom smiled knowingly, and Evie spent the rest of the trip flustered while Cole looked far too pleased with himself.



✨ These five show how their friendship is ordinary, cozy, and real — but always with that spark outsiders mistake for romance.

Grace’s middle school dance. Evie and Cole stood together by the refreshment table, gently teasing kids who tried to sneak extra sodas. “You two are the cutest chaperones,” one mom whispered. Grace told her friends, “That’s basically my aunt and uncle.” Evie turned bright red; Cole just smirked.

15. Christmas Lights Drive

One December night, Cole drove his truck while Evie held thermoses of cocoa. Grace and Jazz were bundled in the back seat, squealing every time they saw a house covered in lights.

Neighbors waved as the truck rolled slowly past, assuming it was a family outing. Evie leaned back in her seat, cheeks glowing from the cocoa and the lights. Cole glanced sideways, quietly thinking he didn’t mind the assumption at all.

Storyline: Addie & Cole — The Evie Question

1. Addie Notices the Pattern

At a BSS dinner, Addie watches Evie and Cole arrive together again. She sees the comfort — the way Cole automatically pours her water, the way Evie laughs more easily with him nearby. Later that night, Addie corners Cole by the bar.

“So,” she says casually, “when are you going to tell me what’s really going on with you and Evie?”

Cole shakes his head. “Nothing to tell. She’s a friend.”

Addie gives him the kind of look only Addie can — half amusement, half steel. “Friends don’t look at each other like that, Cole. Not unless there’s more under the surface.”



2. Addie Offers Her Support

Over the next few weeks, Addie asks again — never prying, just nudging. Each time Cole insists it’s friendship, but Addie doesn’t let it go.

Finally, one evening at her ranch after the kids are asleep, she sits across from Cole by the fire.“You cleared paths for me when I needed them,” she says softly. “Made things possible I couldn’t have managed alone. If Evie is someone who matters to you — even if it’s just as a friend — I’ll do the same for you. Whatever you need.”

Cole doesn’t answer immediately. He just stares into the fire, jaw set. Addie doesn’t push, but he knows she means it.



3. Addie Subtly Shields Evie

Addie starts weaving quiet support around Evie without ever making it obvious:

Ensuring Evie always has a place at events where she’d otherwise feel out of place.

Pairing her with friendly donors or allies instead of sharks.

Making sure when whispers start about “Evie and Cole,” they never carry a cruel edge.

When Evie asks why she’s always so included, Addie just smiles. “Because you belong here.”



4. Addie Confronts Cole (Gently)

Months later, after a Strong & Savory fundraiser, Addie catches Cole outside. “You’re still standing at the edge, Cole,” she says. “Not stepping forward, not stepping back. Is that fair to her?”

Cole exhales. “She doesn’t need me in her life like that. She’s… good. Untouched by all this. I won’t pull her into my mess.”

Addie studies him for a long moment. “That’s what you said about me once. Remember how that turned out?”

Cole doesn’t reply, but the look in his eyes says he hasn’t forgotten.



5. Addie’s Quiet Promise

Later, talking with Hawk, Addie admits, “Evie may not even know it, but she’s part of us now. Cole won’t say it, but she steadies him. I’ll keep her safe — for him, for Grace, for the kids. And if one day they figure it out for themselves… well, the path will already be clear.”



✨ Canon Anchor:

Addie becomes the protector and advocate of Evie & Cole’s bond, much like Cole once was for her.

She doesn’t force anything — she just makes space, offers cover, and reminds Cole that Evie belongs.

Scene — Evie & Addie, Kitchen Table at the Ranch

The ranch was quiet after dinner. Hawk had taken the kids outside to chase fireflies, leaving Addie and Evie in the big farmhouse kitchen with mugs of tea. The counter still smelled faintly of grilled corn and peach cobbler.

Evie looked down into her cup, fingers tracing the rim. “Addie… I just wanted to say thank you. You’ve been… so kind to me. Including me in things, making sure I don’t get lost in the shuffle. You didn’t have to.”

Addie smiled faintly, resting her chin on her hand. “Evie, you don’t get lost in the shuffle. Not with the way Grace talks about you. Or the way Cole looks at you.”

Evie flushed, shaking her head. “That’s just it. I don’t want a relationship, Addie. The kids — they’re my relationship. They’re enough. But… it’s nice having Cole around. A friend to go to the market with or laugh at movies. Someone outside the kitchen. He makes me feel like I have a life that isn’t just flour and sugar.”

Addie’s gaze softened. “You know, when I met Cole, I was broken. Hospital bed, machines humming, everything falling apart. He didn’t just save me — he introduced me to Hawk. Changed everything. And I’ve never forgotten that.”

Evie looked up, surprised. Addie reached across the table, squeezing her hand gently. “So hear me clearly: whatever happens between you and Cole — or doesn’t — you’re family now. Grace adores you. She calls you her niece’s teacher, but really, she just sees you as hers. And with that, you’ll always have protection. Always.”

Evie tilted her head, searching Addie’s expression. “Protection? That… means something more, doesn’t it?”

Addie’s smile turned just a touch mysterious. “Let’s just say there are circles you don’t need to know about to benefit from. Circles that look after their own. And you’re one of ours now, Evie. That’s not going to change.”

For a moment, Evie didn’t speak. She just sat there, stunned by the weight of the words, the finality of them. She had always felt on the outside looking in, even when Cole or Sara drew her into the orbit. But Addie’s tone carried no doubt, no hesitation.

“I don’t know what to say,” Evie whispered finally.

“Say nothing,” Addie replied warmly. “Just keep baking. Keep teaching. Keep being you. That’s all we need.”

Outside, Grace’s laughter rang through the night. Evie looked toward the sound, her chest tightening with a quiet sense of belonging she had never felt before.

Scene — Evie & Sara at the Gym Office

The gym was quiet after evening classes, the hum of treadmills finally stilled. Evie lingered in Sara’s office, perched on the edge of the desk with a protein shake half-finished in her hand.

She hesitated before speaking. “Sara… Addie said something the other night at the ranch. She told me I was family now. That I’d always have protection. And she said it like it… meant more than just being nice.”

Sara set down her clipboard, eyes softening. “That sounds like Addie.”

Evie leaned forward. “What does it mean? Protection? She didn’t explain. Just said I was one of you now.”

Sara smiled faintly, folding her arms. “Evie, there are some things Addie carries that don’t need explaining. But this much I’ll tell you: she’s important. To a lot of people, in ways most will never see. And when she says someone is family, she means it.”

Evie frowned. “But… she made it sound like she’d risk something. Like she’d lose everything if she had to.”

Sara nodded slowly. “That’s Addie. She takes care of her own, no matter the cost. Always has, always will. It’s who she is.”

Evie sat back, quiet for a long moment. The words settled over her, heavy but strangely comforting. She didn’t fully understand — but maybe she didn’t need to.

Sara gave her a gentle smile. “You don’t have to worry about the details. Just know this: if Addie said you’re family, then you’re safe. That’s the only part that matters.”

Evie exhaled, tension loosening in her shoulders. For the first time in a long time, she let herself believe she truly belonged.

Scene — Movie Theater & 24-Hour Diner

The movie had been Evie’s pick, of course — some indie rom-com Cole had pretended to hate but secretly laughed at in the dark theater. Now it was past midnight, and they sat across from each other in a booth at the all-night diner, half a plate of pancakes between them, Cole sipping black coffee while Evie drowned hers in syrup.

Evie twirled her fork idly, her voice softer than usual. “Addie told me something the other night.”

Cole raised an eyebrow. “That’s dangerous.”

She smiled, but the seriousness lingered. “She said I’m family now. That I’ll always have protection. And she said it like… it was a vow. Like she’d risk everything to make it true.”

Cole’s expression shifted — not surprise, but something close to respect. He leaned back, folding his arms. “That sounds like her.”

Evie studied him across the table. “Cole… what does that mean? Protection? It felt heavier than she let on.”

He was quiet for a long moment, staring into his coffee. Finally, he said, “It means exactly what it sounded like. Addie doesn’t make casual promises. If she says you’re family, she’d burn down walls to keep you safe. I’ve seen her do it.”

Evie tilted her head. “She hinted you did the same for her once.”

Cole’s jaw flexed, but he didn’t deny it. “Different time. Different mess. But she’s not wrong.”

Evie set her fork down, pushing the plate aside. “I don’t know why she’d say it to me. I’m just… a teacher who bakes too much.”

Cole’s eyes lifted to hers, steady and unflinching. “You’re not ‘just’ anything, Evie. You matter. To the kids, to Sara, to Grace. And now to Addie. That’s enough reason.”

She blinked, cheeks warming, her chest tight with the weight of his certainty. For a moment, she didn’t know what to say.

So instead, she laughed softly, trying to lighten it. “You always know how to ruin pancakes with serious speeches.”

Cole smirked faintly. “Then eat faster.”

And just like that, the heaviness dissolved into easy laughter again. But deep down, Evie carried the truth with her — that whatever “protection” meant in Addie and Cole’s world, she was no longer on the outside looking in.

Scene — Movie Theater & 24-Hour Diner

The neon sign buzzed faintly outside as the diner’s clock crept past 12:30. Evie sat across from Cole in a red vinyl booth, her plate of pancakes soaked in syrup, his coffee still steaming in front of him. They’d just left the theater — her pick, a rom-com Cole pretended to hate but had actually laughed through.

Cole’s brow furrowed, but there was no surprise. Only recognition. “That’s Addie.”

Evie tilted her head, voice softer. “But why me? I’m not… like the rest of you. I’m not running a business, I’m not saving the world. I’m just baking cookies and teaching kids.”

Cole leaned back, arms folding loosely across his chest. His tone was calm, even, but his eyes held steady on hers. “It’s not about titles or what you do. It’s about closeness. Addie protects family. That’s her law.”

He paused, and for a rare moment, let the words weigh heavier. “I was once close to her like that. I was her protector. And she didn’t forget it. Now she’s passing that on to you.”

Evie blinked, stunned. “Her protector?”

Cole gave a faint nod, not elaborating, but his expression said enough — a history, unspoken, but carved deep.

Evie sat back slowly, processing. Her cheeks flushed, but her voice was steady. “So… she sees me like family because I’m close. Even if I’m not… dating you.”

“Exactly,” Cole said. “Dating doesn’t matter. Closeness does. And you’ve earned that with the way you live. The way you show up. That’s why she’ll cover you. No questions asked.”

Then she smiled faintly, trying to lighten it. “Well. Remind me to never cross Addie. Sounds like she’d bulldoze anyone in her way.”

Cole smirked, sipping his coffee. “She would. And now she’d do it for you.”

Evie picked her fork back up, cheeks warm. The pancakes had gone cold, but somehow, they tasted sweeter.

Scene — Evie & Grace by the Firepit (Jasper’s House)

The evening had wound down. Most of the adults had drifted inside Jasper’s estate, the last echoes of laughter fading into the great hall. Outside, the firepit still glowed, embers casting soft light over the patio. Evie lingered there, cardigan wrapped around her, staring into the flames.

She didn’t notice Grace until the girl padded over, hoodie zipped up, marshmallow stick still in her hand. Grace plopped into the chair beside her with the easy confidence of family.

For a while, they sat in silence, the crackle of the fire their only company. Then Grace spoke without looking up.

“Aunt Addie really likes you.”

Evie turned her head. “She does?”

Grace nodded, poking at the ashes. “She talks about you like you’re one of us. And if Aunt Addie says that, it means you are. No take-backs.”

Evie’s throat tightened. “She told me something like that the other night. That I was family. But I… I don’t know why.”

Grace finally looked up, her expression clear, matter-of-fact. “Because that’s what she does. She protects family. Even if you don’t ask for it. Even if it costs her.”

Evie blinked, startled by the certainty in Grace’s voice. “And you believe that?”

“I don’t just believe it,” Grace said firmly. “I’ve seen it. And now you’ll see it too. You’re stuck with us.”

The girl leaned back, folding her arms with the kind of finality only Grace could pull off.

Evie laughed softly, though her eyes shimmered. “Well… if I’m stuck, at least I’ve got good company.”

Grace grinned, satisfied. “Exactly. And besides, I love your classes. You’re already my favorite.”

Evie looked back at the fire, warmth spreading in her chest that had nothing to do with the flames. For the first time, she let herself believe it — that Addie’s promise, Cole’s words, and Grace’s certainty all meant the same thing: she wasn’t on the outside anymore.

She was family.

Scene — Evie’s Reflection

Back in her apartment, the night after Jasper’s dinner, Evie couldn’t sleep. The firepit glow and Grace’s words replayed in her head, circling louder than the quiet hum of her fridge.

She pulled out her old journal — the one with flour smudges on the cover and recipe cards tucked between pages — and sat at her small kitchen table.

On one page, she wrote in her looping, uneven hand:

Addie said I’m family. Cole confirmed it. Grace believes it like it’s the most obvious thing in the world.

She stared at the words, her pen hovering. For so long, she had lived like she had to earn her place — with the kids, with Sara, even with herself. Bake more. Teach more. Smile more. Always prove her worth.

But tonight felt different.

Grace’s voice came back to her: “You’re stuck with us. No take-backs.”

Evie set the pen down, stood, and opened her pantry. Within minutes, the kitchen smelled of cinnamon and butter. Baking was her way of thinking — and this time, every stir of the spoon felt lighter. She wasn’t baking to prove she mattered. She was baking because she was happy.

When the first tray of cookies came out, she set one on a plate, steam curling into the air. She whispered to the empty room, “Family.”

And for the first time, she believed it.

Scene — “Aunt Evie” in Public

The words landed like a stone dropped in water. Everyone smiled, some nodding, no one correcting her.

Evie froze. For a moment, she didn’t know how to respond. She opened her mouth, then closed it, cheeks burning as the firelight caught them. Aunt Evie. No one had ever called her that before.

Cole, sitting across the fire, caught her eye. He gave the smallest nod, steady and sure, as if to say, Yes. That’s exactly what you are.

Evie looked down, twisting her fingers together, but a small, stunned smile tugged at her lips. She didn’t protest. She couldn’t.

Because deep down, hearing it out loud — from Grace, in front of everyone — felt truer than anything she could have said herself.

Scene — Asking Addie for Help

The party at Jasper’s had ended, but Evie lingered in her mind long after. Sitting at her small kitchen table later that week, she couldn’t shake the image of herself in her plain black dress — the nicest thing she owned — standing among women who looked like they belonged on magazine covers.

So the next time she was at Addie’s ranch, she pulled Addie aside, heart thudding.

“Addie… can I ask you something a little embarrassing?”

Addie smiled, warm and knowing. “Of course.”

Evie hesitated, twisting her hands. “At the last gala… I felt underdressed. Honestly, that was the nicest thing I own. And I don’t want to stand out for the wrong reasons. Would you… help me? With what to wear?”

Addie didn’t hesitate. “Yes. Absolutely. We’ll go together. And Evie?” Her smile turned protective, fierce. “I’ll pay for your outfit. Two, maybe three. Whatever you need. You won’t ever have to feel like you don’t belong again.”

Evie’s eyes stung. “Addie, I can’t let you—”

“You can. And you will,” Addie cut her off gently. “Because you’re family, and family doesn’t show up in borrowed wings. We make sure you fly in your own.”

Evie laughed through her tears, shaking her head. “You really don’t take no for an answer, do you?”

Addie’s grin widened. “Never.”

Just then, Grace burst into the room, half-eaten cookie in hand. “What are we talking about?”

Addie winked at Evie. “Your favorite thing. Shopping.”

Grace’s eyes lit up. “I’m coming.”

Addie nodded. “Of course. I never pick an outfit without my best consultant.”

Evie’s nervous laugh softened into a smile as Grace spun around in excitement. For the first time, Evie felt like maybe — just maybe — she could belong in that glittering world after all.

Scene — Shopping with Addie & Grace

The boutique gleamed with mirrors and chandeliers, racks of gowns shimmering like they belonged in another universe. Evie hesitated in the doorway, cardigan pulled tight, but Addie’s hand on her shoulder kept her moving. Grace darted ahead, already tugging dresses from racks with the precision of someone who had watched Addie shop a hundred times.

“Okay,” Grace declared, “first up: queen dress.” She shoved a midnight-blue gown into Evie’s hands.

Evie disappeared into the dressing room and stepped out moments later. The dress hugged her waist, the fabric pooling like water around her feet. She looked into the mirror and gasped — regal, almost intimidating. Addie’s smile said it all.

“See?” Addie murmured. “That’s how the world should see you.”

Next came the sexy dress — scarlet, cut low at the back. Evie emerged nervously, cheeks burning. Grace’s jaw dropped. “Uncle Cole would be crazy not to fall for you in that.”

Evie nearly tripped on the hem. Addie burst out laughing, Grace cackling beside her. Evie threw up her hands. “Okay, next dress, please!”

Finally, Evie slipped into a soft sage-green gown — simple, elegant, understated. She looked like herself, but elevated. Addie nodded with approval. “That one’s your anchor. Low key, but you’ll fit in without losing who you are.”

By the end of the afternoon, Addie had decided: one of each. Queen. Sexy. Low key. Plus a few extra “everyday” dresses that still shimmered enough to turn heads.

Then came shoes and accessories. Grace insisted on matching heels for each gown — silver, black, and nude — plus a delicate necklace that sparkled without being ostentatious. Evie tried not to wince every time she glimpsed a price tag.

At the register, she whispered, “Addie, this is too much. I can’t let you—”

Addie waved her off, sliding her card across the counter like it was nothing. “Evie, trust me. This is nothing compared to what my network has done for me. Most of these things don’t even come out of my pocket. And even if they did — you’re worth it.”

Evie swallowed hard, torn between gratitude and guilt. She had always prided herself on being self-sufficient, never owing anyone. But the way Addie said it — firm, final, protective — made it clear this wasn’t charity. It was family.

Grace squeezed her hand. “You’re going to look amazing, Aunt Evie.”

Evie laughed, a little dazed, as she glanced at the bags piled around her. For once, she didn’t feel like she was trying to borrow someone else’s wings. She felt like Addie and Grace had helped her grow her own.

Scene — Pre-Gala Ritual

The upstairs suite of Addie’s ranch house was a whirlwind of satin and silk. Racks of gowns lined the walls, shoes in neat rows, jewelry boxes open on the vanity. This was the ritual before every major gala: the women gathering to prepare together, trading dresses, fixing makeup, laughing as though it were just another Friday night.

Evie stood in the doorway, clutching her garment bag, eyes wide. “I… I don’t belong in this world.”

Addie, already half-pinned into a shimmering emerald gown, looked up with a grin. “Oh yes you do. You’re here, aren’t you? That makes it official.” She winked. “Though maybe we need to take you shopping again — this time for proper undergarments.”

Grace darted around the room like a tiny stylist, zipping dresses, handing out earrings, and brushing stray hairs into place. When Evie finally stepped out of her new sage-green gown, Grace stopped in her tracks, eyes wide.

“Aunt Evie,” she breathed, “you look like a princess. Uncle Cole is going to be so surprised.”

Evie pressed her hands to her cheeks, trying not to cry. “Grace… the only other time I’ve ever done anything like this was my prom. And you all do it like it’s just… a Friday night.”

Addie crossed the room, resting a hand on her shoulder. “That’s because it is for us. But now it is for you, too. This is what family looks like. We dress each other up, we laugh, we tease, and then we go take over the room together.”

Evie looked into the mirror again, her reflection almost unrecognizable. The dress, the hair, the necklace Addie had insisted on — it all shimmered like a dream. But what made her glow wasn’t the silk or the jewels. It was the women around her, the child tugging at her sleeve, the warmth of belonging settling in her chest.

She whispered, half to herself, “I’ll never get used to this.”

Addie’s smile in the mirror was soft but sure. “Good. Don’t get used to it. Just enjoy it. Every time.”

In the mirror, Evie hardly recognized herself. But the warmth in the room, the teasing, the way Grace clung to her hand — that was what made her shine.

Scene — Pre-Gala Ritual at the Hotel (Refined)

The hotel suite buzzed with chatter and laughter as gowns were steamed and jewelry was passed hand to hand. Evie ducked into the dressing room, clutching her sage-green gown like it was a shield.

She slipped out of her clothes, folding them neatly, and hesitated. Her undergarments were old — practical, worn thin, the kind of thing no one else was ever supposed to see. She prayed no one would notice.

“Evie,” Addie said softly, “we really should have taken you shopping for proper undergarments.”

From across the suite, Grace called out, “Is Aunt Evie ready yet?!”

Addie winked. “Almost, princess. She’s just learning the ropes.”

Evie laughed nervously, still flushed, but she let Addie zip her into the gown. When she turned toward the mirror, the undergarments were forgotten — replaced by the stunning reflection of a woman who looked every bit like she belonged.

Scene — Princess Moment & Walk to the Gala

When Evie stepped out of the dressing room in the sage-green gown, the whole suite seemed to pause.

Grace dropped the shoes she’d been carrying and clapped her hands. “Aunt Evie, you look like a princess!”

The wives smiled knowingly, Addie’s reflection glowing with approval in the mirror. A few of the women adjusted her necklace, fluffed the skirt, and murmured little words of praise that made Evie’s cheeks burn all over again.

Evie’s voice cracked. “This feels… unreal. The only other time I’ve ever dressed up like this was my prom. And you all do it like it’s just… a Friday night.”

Addie rested a hand on her shoulder. “That’s exactly the point. You belong here now. Prom was one night. This is family.”

The women swept out together in their gowns, glittering like queens, with Grace skipping ahead, already rehearsing who would get the first twirl on the ballroom floor. Evie lagged behind with Addie, clutching her little clutch bag like it might steady her heart.

“Addie,” she whispered, voice low, “I was so embarrassed earlier. You seeing me— like that. Those ratty things. I’ve never been more out of place.”

Addie gave a warm laugh, looping her arm through Evie’s as they walked. “Evie, first of all — you’re radiant. Not one person in that ballroom will remember what you wore underneath. And second — we’re going shopping. Non-negotiable.”

Evie groaned softly, though a smile tugged at her mouth. “I’ll never live this down, will I?”

“Nope,” Addie said cheerfully. Then her voice softened, turning thoughtful. “You know who I want you to meet? Bella. She’s a lot like you, more than you realize. Humble, hardworking, self-sufficient. She’ll get you, Evie. And she’s learned how to balance being herself while living in this world. She’ll help.”

Evie blinked. “She’d want to?”

Addie squeezed her arm gently. “She will. Because you’re family now. And once Bella decides you’re one of hers, you’ll never go without backup again.”

They reached the top of the grand staircase leading down to the gala. Evie paused, heart hammering as the ballroom lights shimmered below. Addie leaned close, whispering like a secret.

“Tonight isn’t about dresses, or shoes, or even undergarments. It’s about standing in your own place. And trust me — you’ve earned it.”

Evie drew a breath, lifted her chin, and took her first step down.

Scene — Gala Night

The ballroom glittered with chandeliers, music drifting from the string quartet. At the top of the staircase, Evie hesitated in her sage-green gown, heart pounding. Addie gave her a tiny nod of encouragement, then swept forward to take her place as the quiet center of the room.

Evie took her first step.

At the base of the stairs, Cole glanced up — and stilled. His usual reserved expression cracked for just a second, eyes widening, breath caught. He didn’t move, didn’t smirk, didn’t deflect. He simply looked at her, as if he hadn’t expected to be struck silent by someone he saw every week in flour-dusted cardigans.

Evie flushed under the weight of his gaze, gripping her clutch tighter, forcing herself to keep walking. When she finally reached the bottom, Cole leaned close, voice low so only she could hear.

“Girl-next-door,” he murmured, “and somehow you just outshone every queen in this room.”

Her cheeks burned hotter than the ballroom lights. She managed a shaky laugh. “It’s just the dress.”

Cole shook his head once. “No. It’s you.”



Later, Evie found herself drifting to the edges of the room, catching her breath. From a distance, she watched Addie — poised, luminous, every guest orbiting her with reverence. And yet, even as Addie commanded conversations, Evie noticed her eyes flicker across the room, checking, always aware.

She’s POH, Evie thought suddenly. She didn’t understand all it meant, but she felt it — the weight, the command, the unseen authority. And even in that role, Addie hadn’t stopped looking out for her.



As the night wound down, the men gathered near the bar for a round of drinks. Evie stood awkwardly near the edge of the crowd until Addie appeared at her side, graceful as ever.

“There’s someone you need to meet,” Addie said.

She guided Evie across the room, weaving through glittering gowns and polished laughter, until they reached two women deep in conversation. Bella turned first, her presence warm and grounding, a quiet strength in her eyes. Mandy leaned against the bar beside her, laughing softly at something Bella had said.

“Bella,” Addie said smoothly, “this is Evie. She’s family.”

Bella’s smile widened instantly. She reached out, clasping Evie’s hand warmly. “Then you’re mine, too.”

Mandy grinned and nodded, her approval unspoken but clear.

Evie blinked, overwhelmed, but in the glow of Bella’s welcome and Addie’s steady hand on her back, she felt something settle deep inside. This wasn’t just a dress, or a night, or a borrowed role.

She was being woven in — thread by thread — for good.

Scene — Evie & Bella, Coffee After the Gala

Two days after the gala, Evie sat nervously at a café near the park, fiddling with the lid of her latte. Bella slid into the seat across from her, hair pulled back, her presence calm but warm.

“I’m glad Addie introduced us,” Bella said. “She was right — we’re more alike than you think.”

Evie smiled shyly. “I’m not sure anyone is like me.”

Bella laughed softly. “That’s exactly what I thought when I first stepped into this world. But Addie… she took me in. She taught me everything, like My Fair Lady.”

Evie’s eyes widened. “That’s my favorite book!”

Bella’s grin turned conspiratorial. “Then you’ll understand. I was shy, reserved, completely unsure. Addie tested me, challenged me, pushed me to grow. And when I stumbled, she never gave up — she gave me jobs, opportunities, a place at her side. She welcomed me when I felt invisible.”

Evie’s chest tightened. “That sounds… exactly like her.”

Bella nodded, her tone softening. “And Mandy helped me too. Our relationship opened doors I didn’t even know were closed — to my sexuality, my confidence, my ability to live fully. And then I met Matt, and… everything blossomed. I stopped feeling small. I stopped apologizing for existing.”

Evie laughed nervously. “I can’t even imagine being that bold.”

Bella leaned forward, eyes sparkling. “You don’t have to imagine. Addie sees it in you already. And so do I. You just need time.”

Evie ducked her head, cheeks warm. “She already wants to drag me lingerie shopping after what happened at the hotel.”

Bella’s laughter rang through the café, bright and unrestrained. “Then count me in. If anyone deserves a proper upgrade, it’s you. And I promise, I’ll make it fun.”

Evie covered her face with her hands, laughing despite herself. For the first time since the gala, she didn’t feel like she was stumbling into someone else’s world. She felt like she was making a friend who had walked the path before her — and was ready to walk it again, this time at her side.

Scene — The Lingerie Rite of Passage

The boutique was gleaming, lined with satin, lace, and silk. Evie clutched her cardigan tighter, wishing she could disappear into the floor. She had never set foot in a place like this — not for herself.

Addie marched ahead confidently, Bella at her side, Grace skipping between racks as if she belonged there. “Evie,” Addie said matter-of-factly, “you’ve survived a gala. Now it’s time for the foundation pieces.”

Evie’s face went crimson. “Foundation pieces?”

Bella grinned, holding up a delicate lace set. “Translation: lingerie. You need at least a drawer full.”

Evie sputtered. “A drawer?!”

Addie’s smile was wicked. “Yes. And by the time we’re done, you’ll have one. Consider it a gift from your sisters.”



The Dressing Room Gauntlet

Evie stepped into the dressing room, closing the curtain tight. The first set felt alien on her skin — soft, delicate, almost too beautiful to be hers.

“Come on, Evie, let’s see it!” Bella called, her voice playful.

Evie cracked the curtain an inch. “I can’t. This is ridiculous.”

Addie appeared before she could protest, sweeping inside like she owned the space. “Ridiculous? You look stunning. Don’t hide from us.” She adjusted a strap, her movements brisk but kind. “These aren’t just clothes, Evie. They’re confidence. Armor.”

Evie whispered, “I’ve never done this. I’ve never had… friends like this.”

Bella leaned in through the curtain, eyes soft. “That’s why we’re here. You’re not alone anymore.”



Embarrassment & Laughter

Each time Evie emerged, she tugged at straps, trying to cover herself, cheeks burning. Grace squealed once, clapping her hands: “Aunt Evie, you’re a model!”

Evie nearly dove back into the dressing room. “Grace! You’re not supposed to be—”

Addie laughed, pulling Grace back gently. “Relax. She’s seen me a hundred times. This is just another day in the sisterhood.”

Bella gave a mock bow. “Welcome to initiation, darling. Everyone blushes their first trip.”



The Final Drawer

By the time they were finished, the counter was piled high: lace, silk, satin, colors Evie never would have chosen for herself. Addie signed the bill without blinking, and the clerk boxed everything neatly.

Evie stared at the stack, overwhelmed. “I… I can’t accept all of this.”

Addie’s tone softened, but her eyes stayed firm. “Yes, you can. Because this isn’t about lingerie, Evie. It’s about you finally being surrounded by women who will lift you up, no matter how embarrassed you get. You’ve given everything to the kids, to your teaching. Now it’s your turn.”

Bella looped her arm through Evie’s, grin mischievous. “And I fully expect fashion shows when you’re ready. Sisters’ orders.”

Evie laughed despite herself, a little dazed, a little shy. For the first time in her life, she wasn’t just the teacher, or the baker, or the outsider. She was part of something larger, brighter — and her drawer full of satin and lace was proof she’d never walk alone again.

Midway through the cooking class that followed, Cole passed through the gym floor, towel slung over his shoulder. He glanced into the kitchen space casually — then stopped.

Evie was at the counter, hair pulled back, laughing with a group of kids as she stirred a pot. Her new top fit perfectly, the necklace Addie had chosen glinting under the lights. She looked comfortable, radiant, alive.

Cole didn’t linger, but Bella noticed the way his step slowed, the way his gaze held a moment too long before he forced himself back to the weight rack. She elbowed Addie, who smirked knowingly.

When Evie turned, flushed from the heat of the stove and the laughter of her students, she caught Cole’s retreating figure. Something in her chest tightened, though she quickly pushed it aside.

Bella leaned close, whispering, “Told you. Worth it.”

Evie rolled her eyes, but she was smiling.

Scene — First Look at the Gym

The gym was buzzing on a Saturday morning. Cole walked in from the back entrance, towel over his shoulder, head down as he scrolled through his training notes.

Out of the corner of his eye, he caught a figure leading a warm-up stretch on the mats — sleek black leggings, fitted tank, hair pulled up high. She moved with a confidence that caught him off guard. He almost kept walking, assuming she was one of Sara’s newer trainers.

Then she turned.

It was Evie.

Cole stopped dead in his tracks. For a split second, he didn’t recognize her — not the flour-dusted teacher, not the shy woman tugging cardigans close. This was someone else entirely, someone radiant, almost unreachably confident.

Their eyes met. Evie’s cheeks burned instantly. She stumbled on her words mid-instruction, quickly redirecting the class before fleeing to the back office under the guise of fetching equipment.



Scene — Retreat

For the next week, Evie went back to her usual clothes: loose t-shirts, baggy sweatpants, sneakers that had seen better years. She laughed it off when Bella teased her, insisting she was “just more comfortable this way.”

But alone in her apartment, she opened the drawer Addie had filled for her. Satin, lace, silk — colors she still blushed to touch. She wore them under her plain clothes, every day. No one saw, no one knew.

It wasn’t about anyone else. It was her secret reminder: she could step into that other life if she wanted. She could be the confident woman in the mirror, the one Cole hadn’t even recognized.

For now, she wasn’t ready to live it out loud. But she wasn’t ready to let it go, either.

Scene — Bella & Evie, Sister Talk

The gym was nearly empty late in the evening. Evie sat cross-legged on the edge of the mat, tugging at the hem of her oversized t-shirt, while Bella sipped water and studied her with a knowing smile.

“You’ve gone back,” Bella said softly.

Evie looked up, startled. “Back?”

Bella tilted her head. “Baggy clothes. Hiding. You were radiant last week, Evie. And now you’re retreating.”

Evie’s cheeks flushed. She stared at the floor. “It was too much. Everyone was looking at me. Cole didn’t even recognize me at first. I panicked. This—” She gestured at the loose shirt, the old sneakers. “—this feels safer.”

Bella set her water down, sliding closer. “Safer doesn’t mean better. But I get it. I really do.”

Evie hesitated, then admitted in a whisper, “I didn’t stop completely. I still wear… what Addie bought me. Underneath. Every day. It’s like… a reminder that maybe I could be her, the woman in the mirror. If I wanted to.”

Bella smiled, eyes soft. “That’s more than enough for now. You don’t have to prove anything to me, or to Addie, or to Cole. You wear what makes you feel strong, even if it’s just underneath. That’s your choice, your pace.”

Evie blinked, her eyes shimmering. “You’re not… disappointed in me?”

Bella shook her head, slipping an arm around her shoulders. “Never. I’ve got your back one-thousand percent, no matter what. You are always welcome in this life — but don’t think that means you can escape me or Addie, either.” Her grin turned mischievous. “We’ll always be checking in. Sisters’ privilege.”

Evie laughed through her tears, leaning into her. “You two really don’t let up, do you?”

“Nope,” Bella said warmly. “That’s what sisters are for.”

And for the first time in a long while, Evie didn’t feel ashamed of her retreat. She felt held, exactly as she was — not pressured to bloom faster, but reminded that when she was ready, she’d never have to walk that path alone.

Scene — Bella & Evie, Sister Talk (Expanded)

The gym lights buzzed faintly overhead, most of the equipment already racked for the night. Evie sat cross-legged on the edge of the mat, tugging nervously at the hem of her oversized t-shirt. Bella, ever unhurried, lowered herself beside her, water bottle balanced on her knee.

“You’ve gone back,” Bella said gently.

Evie blinked. “Back?”

Bella tilted her head, smiling knowingly. “The baggy clothes. Hiding. Last week you walked in radiant. Today you’re drowning yourself in fabric again.”

Evie’s shoulders slumped. “It was too much, Bella. Everyone was looking at me. And when Cole saw me… he didn’t even recognize me at first. And then when he did—” She swallowed. “The way he reacted felt like something more. More than friendship. And I… I can’t handle a relationship right now.”

Bella’s expression softened. She reached over, brushing Evie’s hand. “You don’t have to. No one’s asking you to. Cole least of all.”

Evie exhaled shakily, lowering her gaze. “I don’t want to hurt him. But I don’t want to lead him on either.”

Bella nodded. “Then don’t. Just keep being you. He’s steady enough to handle it. And if his feelings grow deeper, that’s his to carry — not yours to fix. You’re allowed to set the pace of your own life.”

For a long moment, Evie was quiet. Then, in a whisper: “I didn’t stop completely. I still wear… what Addie bought me. Underneath. Every day. It feels like… a reminder that maybe I could be her. The woman in the mirror. If I wanted to.”

Bella’s smile turned tender. “That’s more than enough. You don’t need to live someone else’s timeline, Evie. And don’t think for a second that retreating means we’re disappointed. I’ve got your back a thousand percent, no matter what.”

Evie looked at her, eyes shimmering. “Really?”

“Really,” Bella said firmly. Then her grin turned sly. “But don’t think that means you can escape me or Addie, either. We’ll always be checking in. Sisters’ privilege.”

Evie laughed through her tears, leaning into her shoulder. “You two don’t let up.”

“Nope,” Bella murmured. “And that’s how you’ll know you’re never alone.”

Scene — Cole & Evie, Quiet Conversation

The gym had emptied out after the evening rush. Evie lingered in the cooking space, wiping down counters that were already spotless, trying to summon the courage. Cole passed through, towel draped over his shoulder, pausing when he noticed her.

“You’re still here?” he asked.

Evie hesitated, then nodded. “I… needed to talk to you. About last week.”

Cole raised an eyebrow, leaning against the counter, waiting.

She took a breath. “Addie and Bella took me on a… girls’ day. Dresses, shoes, lingerie—everything. They made me feel like someone else for a little while. Like I belonged. And when I came into the gym in those new clothes, you…” She faltered. “You didn’t recognize me at first.”

Cole’s gaze was steady. “You looked different. Radiant. It caught me off guard.”

Evie’s cheeks warmed, but she forced herself to continue. “That’s the problem. The way you reacted—it felt like more than friendship. And I can’t… I can’t handle more right now. I’m not ready. The kids, my teaching, this life—that’s where all my heart is. I don’t want to hurt you, but I don’t want to lead you on either.”

Silence stretched between them for a moment. Cole finally pushed off the counter, standing tall but calm.

“I understand,” he said simply. “More than you think. You don’t owe me anything, Evie. Not romance, not explanations. Just honesty. And you’ve given me that.”

Her chest loosened, relief flooding in. “So… we’re okay?”

Cole’s mouth tugged into the faintest smile. “We’re better than okay. Friends, like always. And if that’s all it ever is, I’m good with that.”

Evie blinked, tears pricking her eyes. “Thank you.”

Cole reached out, just briefly squeezing her shoulder. “Don’t thank me. Just keep showing up as you. That’s all I need.”

As he walked away toward the weight racks, Evie exhaled shakily. The weight of the words—her fear, his acceptance—settled into something steady, something she could trust.

For the first time since the gala, she felt like she could breathe again.

Scene — Cole & Evie, the Diner Talk

The gym was almost empty, the hum of treadmills fading as the last members cleared out. Evie wiped the counters in the cooking space, nerves buzzing. Cole stopped on his way past, towel around his shoulders.

“You’re still here?” he asked.

At the Diner

The corner booth was quiet, a half-empty pot of coffee between them, neon light from the window painting the table. Cole leaned back, arms folded loosely, waiting.

Evie fiddled with her mug. “Addie and Bella took me on this… girls’ day. Dresses, shoes, lingerie — everything. They didn’t let me escape a single store. It was overwhelming. Embarrassing. And yet… for a moment, it felt good. Like I belonged.”

She gave a small laugh. “Then Addie sent you pictures. I thought I was going to die of embarrassment.”

Cole’s mouth curved slightly. “I got them. And I didn’t recognize you at first. You looked… different. Like the version of you no one else had seen yet.”

Her face flushed. “That’s why I pulled back. The way you reacted — it felt like more than friendship. And I can’t handle more, Cole. Not now. The kids, my teaching, that’s where my life is. I don’t want to hurt you, but I don’t want to lead you on either.”

He stirred his coffee slowly, letting her words hang. Finally, he nodded. “I hear you. And I respect it.”

Evie’s breath caught. “So… we’re okay?”

Cole leaned forward, steady. “We’re good. Better than good. Friends, Evie. Always.”

He let the silence linger just long enough before adding, “But I do want the details of that girls’ day. All of it. Not just Addie’s version with emojis and Bella’s smart comments.”

Evie laughed, surprised. “You really want to know?”

“Yeah,” Cole said, eyes steady on hers. “Because it mattered to you. And if it mattered to you, then I want to hear it.”

So she told him. About Addie teasing her in the dressing room, about Bella egging her on, about walking out in dresses that made her feel like a stranger and a queen at once. About the necklace that sparkled when she laughed, and the drawer full of things she never thought she’d own.

By the time she finished, her cheeks hurt from smiling. Cole didn’t interrupt once, just listened, the corners of his mouth tugging now and then in quiet amusement.

When the plates were cleared and the coffee drained, Cole finally said, “See? That’s not just clothes. That’s your sisters making sure you know you’re not alone. Don’t run from that.”

Evie’s chest tightened, her throat thick. “I’ll try not to.”

Cole’s answer was simple, firm. “Good. Because I’ll be checking too.”

Scene — Cole & Evie, Diner Talk (Extended)

Evie finished recounting the whirlwind of the girls’ day, cheeks still pink from the memory of stepping out in gowns and lingerie while Addie and Bella cheered. She shook her head, embarrassed and amused all at once. “I don’t know how I survived it.”

Her eyes softened, but Cole wasn’t finished.

“They know you said no. And I’ve said no too. Relationship, romance, all of that — we’ve both made it clear.” He paused, letting the weight of his honesty settle. “But secretly? They want us together. That’s why the outfits, the hints, the galas. It’s the best way they know how to help.”

Evie froze, cheeks warming. “Together? But—”

Cole lifted a hand, steady, reassuring. “Don’t panic. I’m not pushing for anything. I respect your no. I just want you to understand — their teasing isn’t pressure. It’s affection. It’s their way of telling you they truly love you. That they want the best for you. That they want you seen the way they see you.”

Evie stared at him, her chest tight, words caught in her throat. For so long she’d believed she was tolerated, maybe appreciated, but never wanted this way. And here Cole was, laying it bare with the same calm certainty he brought to everything.

Finally, she whispered, “That’s… a lot to take in.”

Cole’s eyes held hers, steady and unflinching. “So take your time. No one’s going anywhere.”

For the first time that night, Evie smiled without embarrassment.

Scene — Evie’s Journal

Late that night, Evie sat at her small kitchen table, a single lamp casting soft light over the page. Her journal lay open, pen in hand.

Cole said something tonight I can’t stop thinking about. He told me Addie and Bella know I said no, and he said no too. But secretly, they want us together. That’s why they push me into dresses, whisper hints, drag me to galas. I thought it was pressure. But Cole said no — it’s love. Their way of saying they truly care, that they want the best for me, that they want me seen the way they see me.

She set the pen down, eyes stinging. For years she had thought of herself as background — the teacher, the helper, never the one anyone celebrated. And now she realized these women weren’t dressing her up to laugh at her. They were lifting her up, giving her a place she hadn’t believed she deserved.

Evie touched the necklace Addie had clasped around her throat during the gala prep, whispering to the empty room: “They love me.”

For the first time, she believed it.



Scene — Lunch with Addie

Two days later, Evie nervously dialed Addie. “Could we… have lunch? Just us?”

“Of course,” Addie said without hesitation.

They met at a quiet café, tucked into a corner booth. Evie fumbled with her napkin, then looked up. “I just wanted to thank you. For everything. The shopping, the gala, the… the way you’ve included me.”

Addie’s smile was warm, patient. “You don’t have to thank me. You’re family. That’s all there is to it.”

Evie shook her head. “No, I do. Because I didn’t understand before. I thought the dresses, the hints, the galas — they were pressure. But now I realize… they were love. Your way of showing I mattered. Cole helped me see that.”

Addie’s expression softened, her eyes bright with pride. She reached across the table, squeezing Evie’s hand. “Good. Then the message got through. Because that’s exactly what it was. Love, plain and simple. We wanted you to see yourself the way we already see you.”

Evie blinked back tears. “I’m starting to.”

Addie squeezed her hand tighter. “Then that’s all I need.”

They sat in comfortable silence for a moment, the noise of the café fading around them. For the first time, Evie didn’t feel like an outsider sitting across from Addie. She felt like a sister, finally seated at the same table.

Scene — Lunch with Addie (Expanded)

The café was quiet, sunlight streaming through tall windows. Evie sat across from Addie, napkin folded tightly in her lap. She had rehearsed this moment a dozen times, but the words still tangled in her chest.

“Addie,” she began, voice soft. “I wanted to thank you. For everything. The shopping, the gala, the way you’ve… pulled me in. I didn’t understand at first. I thought the dresses, the hints, the galas — they were pressure. But now I realize… they were love. Your way of showing I mattered. Cole helped me see that.”

Evie swallowed, blinking back tears. “I’m starting to. But I need to admit something too. After the gala, I… reverted. Back to baggy clothes, safe things. I panicked. The stares, the attention — it was too much. But I didn’t stop completely. I still wear what you bought me. Underneath. It’s like… a reminder. A secret connection to that version of me, if I ever want to step into her again.”

Addie’s expression didn’t change — no disappointment, no frustration, only understanding. She squeezed Evie’s hand tighter. “That’s enough. More than enough. You don’t have to walk into every room in gowns and heels to belong. You belong because you’re family. Clothes can make you feel brave, but they don’t define you. We already see you — radiant, whether you’re in silk or a cardigan.”

Evie’s chest tightened, a tear sliding down her cheek. “You really mean that?”

Addie smiled, warm and unwavering. “I do. And don’t worry about retreating. Bella did the same. I did too, once. It’s normal. What matters is, you don’t have to hide forever. When you’re ready, we’ll be right there. Checking in. Cheering. Loving you through it.”

Evie laughed softly, brushing her cheek. “You really don’t let me off the hook, do you?”

Addie grinned. “Not a chance.”

For the first time, Evie believed she could breathe in both worlds — the safety of her old clothes and the bravery of her new ones — without losing her place at the table Addie had set for her.

Bella’s grin returned, but it was full of pride now, not teasing. “Exactly. Maybe not today, maybe not tomorrow. But one day, you’ll be in gowns and heels without flinching. You’ll be sitting at Addie’s table, not as the newcomer but as the one bringing others in. That’s the future I see.”

Evie’s eyes shimmered, her voice small. “And if I don’t get there?”

Bella squeezed her hand. “Then we’ll still be here. Loving you, pushing you, checking in. But trust me, Evie—you will. Because you’re stronger than you think. And Addie doesn’t miss when she picks her people.”

Mandy raised her cup like a toast, her smile sharp but kind. “To future Evie. Bigger than she knows.”

Evie laughed through her tears, shaking her head. “You two are impossible.”

“Impossible sisters,” Bella corrected, her voice warm. “And now you’re stuck with us.”

Scene — Evie’s Journal

That night, Evie curled up at her kitchen table, pen in hand, her journal open to a blank page. The words tumbled out faster than she could think.

Bella said I already have a larger-than-life aura. Larger-than-life. Me. I can’t stop replaying it. She said it’s time to expand that with sisters, with family, to go bigger.

And Mandy — she said Strong & Savory is just the seed. She sees a foundation in me. Global, lasting, something that could be my legacy. My legacy. I’ve never thought of myself in those terms. But now I can’t get it out of my head.

She paused, staring at the ink as her hand trembled slightly. Then she scrawled the line she’d been avoiding:

And then they joked about the future Mrs. Cole. I laughed it off, I blushed, I said no. But I can’t get it out of my head either. Larger-than-life. A foundation. Mrs. Cole. They’re circling me over and over, and I don’t know what to do with them.

She closed the journal quickly, heart pounding, but the words wouldn’t leave her.



Scene — The Next Friend Date (Cole & Evie, Evening Walk)

Their “friend dates” had become routine — movies, late diners, coffee walks. Tonight, it was a simple stroll through the park, the city lights glowing beyond the trees.

Evie walked beside Cole in silence for a while, twisting her hands. Finally, she blurted, “Bella and Mandy said something the other day. Actually… a few things. And I can’t stop thinking about them.”

Cole glanced at her, calm, patient. “Alright. Tell me.”

She took a shaky breath. “Bella said I already have a larger-than-life aura, and that I should expand it with family and friends, go bigger. Mandy said Strong & Savory is just the seed, that I could have a foundation one day. Something lasting. And then—” She hesitated, cheeks heating. “They joked about the future Mrs. Cole. And I can’t get any of it out of my head.”

Cole was quiet, his steps slow and steady. Finally, he spoke. “Bella’s right. You do have a larger-than-life aura. People light up around you. You don’t see it, but everyone else does. And Mandy’s right too — Strong & Savory is more than a class. It’s the kind of thing that could grow into something much bigger, if you wanted it to.”

Evie’s throat tightened. “And the other thing?”

Cole gave a faint smile, eyes forward. “The Mrs. Cole part? That’s just them being sisters. They love you, and that’s how they show it. Don’t let it scare you. Don’t let it stick unless you want it to.”

Evie let out a shaky laugh, her chest loosening. “You make it sound so simple.”

“That’s because it is,” Cole said. “What you do with all of this? That’s your choice. No one else’s.”

They walked on in silence, Evie’s heart still circling Bella’s vision, Mandy’s legacy, and the joke she couldn’t quite shake — Mrs. Cole.

Scene — Addie Plants the Seed

A few days after her walk with Cole, Evie found herself seated across from Addie again, this time in Addie’s home office. Papers, files, and notes covered the desk, but Addie’s full attention was on her.

“Cole said you’ve been thinking about Bella and Mandy’s comments,” Addie said gently, her tone making it clear she already knew.

Evie nodded, nervous. “They said Strong & Savory could become a foundation one day. That I could… build something lasting. I can’t stop thinking about it, but it feels too big. I’m just me. Just a teacher.”

Addie leaned forward, eyes steady. “Evie, I’ve watched you. You’re not ‘just’ anything. You’ve created something powerful without even trying. Strong & Savory isn’t just a class — it’s a blueprint. With the right support, it could become an institution. And if you want it, I can help you get there.”

Evie blinked. “Help me… how?”

Addie reached into a folder and began sliding papers across the desk. Names. Numbers. Organizations. “These are contacts — people who specialize in foundations, funding, legal structures, education networks. People who owe me favors. If you wanted to build this tomorrow, you’d have the team to do it.”

Evie stared at the list, overwhelmed. “This is… too much. I don’t even know where to start.”

Addie smiled softly. “That’s why you have me. And Bella. And Mandy. You don’t have to do it alone. You were never meant to.”

Evie swallowed hard, tears pricking her eyes. “Addie… why me?”

Addie’s expression warmed, her voice quiet but firm. “Because you’re already doing the work with nothing but your heart and a handful of pans. Imagine what you could do with resources, with a structure, with people backing you. I see it, Evie. They see it. One day, you’ll see it too.”

Evie pressed a hand to her chest, struggling to breathe under the weight of it all. “I’m not ready.”

“Good,” Addie said with a small grin. “That means you’ll do it the right way — slow, thoughtful, intentional. But know this: when you are ready, the path is already cleared. And you’ll never walk it alone.”

Evie left with the folder clutched in her arms, her mind spinning with names and possibilities she had never imagined for herself. She was terrified. But for the first time, she also believed Addie might be right.

Scene — Bella Helps Evie Sort the Folder

Evie sat on her couch, the heavy folder Addie had given her spread open on the coffee table. Names, numbers, organizational charts, handwritten notes — all spilling across the surface like a wave she couldn’t contain.

She rubbed her temples. “This is insane. I don’t even know what half of these mean.”

Evie groaned, flopping back against the couch. “I can’t do this. It’s too much. I’m not—”

Or even this year.” She shuffled through the stack, pulling out three pages and sliding the rest aside. “Look. Here’s what you need right now: one mentor, one funding contact, one legal advisor. Forget the rest until you’re ready.”

Evie laughed through her nerves, shaking her head. “You’re impossible.”

For the first time, Evie didn’t feel crushed by the folder. She felt anchored. Addie had given her the vision. Bella was giving her the roadmap. And somewhere in between, she realized, maybe she could actually do this.

Scene — Dinner with Bella & Mandy (Chinese Takeout & Ice Cream)

The coffee table was covered in open cartons of Chinese takeout: lo mein spilling onto the lid, half-eaten dumplings, orange chicken cooling in a corner. Next to them sat two pints of ice cream with spoons sticking out, the perfect counterbalance to the salty feast.

Bella leaned back against the couch cushions, chopsticks dangling lazily in her hand. Mandy sprawled in the armchair, carton in her lap, and Evie sat cross-legged on the floor, ice cream pint balanced between her knees.

“You know,” Mandy said between bites of lo mein, “you’re lucky. Bella’s signing up to mentor you now, but when she was learning the ropes? She had me.”

Bella groaned, covering her face. “Mandy…”

Mandy grinned wickedly. “Oh, come on. She was shy. Reserved. Couldn’t look people in the eye half the time. I had to drag her out of that shell with my… playful style.” She winked. “And yes, we dated. And more.”

Evie almost dropped her spoon. “Wait—what?!”

Bella threw a dumpling wrapper at Mandy, laughing despite herself. “Do you have to?”

“Yes,” Mandy said immediately, smirking. “Because it’s true. And because you’re sitting here acting like you were born ready for gowns and galas. Please. I had to coach you through half of it. You didn’t even know how to order wine on our first date.”

Evie giggled, shaking her head. “That sounds exactly like me right now.”

Bella sighed, but her smile was soft. “She’s not wrong. Mandy pushed me. She teased, tested, forced me out of hiding. And she’s still doing it — just ask Matt.”

Mandy raised her carton in mock salute. “Damn right. And now you’ve got the best of both worlds, Evie. Bella, who’ll guide you steady, like Hawk did for Addie. And me, who’ll keep it light and playful, and make sure you don’t forget to laugh while you’re building something big.”

Evie scooped another spoonful of ice cream, cheeks warm, but her grin wide. “I never thought I’d end up with… sisters like you two.”

Bella nudged her knee with her foot, voice warm. “Well, believe it. You’re stuck with us now.”

Mandy grinned, lifting her chopsticks like a toast. “To takeout, ice cream, and no escape.”

All three burst out laughing, the heaviness of visions and futures dissolving into the simple comfort of greasy food, melting ice cream, and the unshakable bond of sisterhood.

Scene — Takeout, Ice Cream & Storytelling

The coffee table was a mess of cartons, soy sauce packets, and melting ice cream. Bella sat curled into the couch cushions, chopsticks dangling idly in her hand. Mandy lounged in the armchair, carton balanced on her lap, while Evie sat on the floor, pint of cookie dough ice cream tucked against her knees.

Mandy smirked as she speared the last dumpling. “You know, Evie, Addie and Elena had their hands full when they got ahold of Bella. They built her into their own little My Fair Lady project. Lessons on clothes, manners, how to walk into a gala like she owned it. They made her polish.”

Bella rolled her eyes, though she was smiling. “It wasn’t that dramatic.”

“Yes, it was,” Mandy fired back. “But while they were making you elegant, I was the one getting you into the spicy side of things. I sharpened your taste. Taught you not just how to show up, but how to enjoy it. How to play.”

Evie’s eyes widened. “Wait — so Addie and Elena made you into ‘lady Bella,’ and you…” She pointed at Mandy. “You made her—”

Mandy grinned, leaning back. “Fun. Adventurous. Less pearls, more fire.”

Bella laughed, shaking her head. “She’s not wrong. Addie and Elena gave me refinement. Mandy gave me edge. And together… they made me who I am now.”

Evie bit her lip, then blurted, “So what was it like? You two dating? More than dating?”

Bella shot Mandy a look, but Mandy was already smirking. “Oh, she wants details.”

Bella sighed, but the corners of her mouth curved up. “It was… intense. She teased me constantly. Dragged me into things I never thought I’d try. And it worked — I stopped hiding. I found my voice.”

Mandy grinned, adding, “She was shy but sharp underneath. I just had to poke until it came out. We had fun — a lot of fun. Nights out, trips, quiet moments that were anything but quiet.”

Evie’s cheeks burned. “Oh my God.”

Bella laughed, covering her face. “See what I mean? She doesn’t hold back.”

Mandy winked. “Why would I? It was good. We were good. But…” Her tone softened. “We broke up because of kids. Bella wanted a future with family, and I wasn’t there yet. Still not, really. We loved each other, but our paths didn’t line up. So we let go before it broke us.”

Bella nodded, her smile tinged with sadness but steady. “It hurt. But it was the right call. And now—” She glanced at Mandy, her eyes warm. “We get to keep the best part. Friendship. Family. And Matt fits her better than I ever could’ve.”

Mandy raised her chopsticks like a toast. “Exactly. We may have broken up, but she’ll always be my girl.”

Evie blinked at both of them, heart tight. “That’s… kind of amazing. You two are so open about it.”

Bella leaned forward, nudging her shoulder. “That’s what sisters are for. The past just makes the present richer. And now you’re part of it.”

Mandy grinned. “No escape.”

Evie laughed, her cheeks hot but her heart full. Somewhere between the takeout, the ice cream, and the stories, she realized she wasn’t just listening to their past. She was being woven into it.

Scene — Evie’s Admission

The cartons were mostly empty now, chopsticks abandoned, and the three women lounged in that comfortable after-dinner haze. Bella had tucked her legs beneath her on the couch, Mandy sprawled like a queen in the armchair, and Evie hugged a pillow to her chest on the floor.

Evie hesitated, then took a shaky breath. “There’s something I’ve never said out loud. Sometimes, when I write in my journal, I admit to myself… I just want to play it small. Go back to the simple life. Teaching kids every day. That makes me happier than… than all of this.” She gestured weakly toward the folder Addie had given her. “The gowns, the galas, the hints, the bigger dreams. Sometimes it feels like a life you’ve given me, not one I chose.”

For a moment, the room was quiet. Then Bella leaned forward, her expression firm but warm. “Evie. Stop right there. None of this was given to you. You earned it. Addie didn’t pluck you out of nowhere — she saw what you were already doing. Loving kids. Teaching. Building Strong & Savory with nothing but heart. All we did was shine a light on what was already there.”

Evie’s eyes shimmered. “But it’s still so big. Too big.”

Bella reached out, squeezing her hand. “Then we take it slow. Step by step. You set the pace, just like Hawk did with Addie. And if your focus stays on the kids? Good. That’s the right foundation. It’s not about trading simple for glamorous. It’s about growing what you already love, without losing yourself.”

Mandy leaned forward, grinning but softer than usual. “Exactly. No one’s asking you to drop everything and run a gala circuit tomorrow. You like teaching kids? Then keep teaching. That’s your heartbeat. The rest can wait. And when you’re ready? We’ll still be here. Teasing. Pushing. Loving. Because you don’t get to run from us either.”

Evie let out a small, shaky laugh, brushing her eyes. “You two don’t make it easy, you know that?”

Bella smiled. “It’s not supposed to be easy. But it will always be yours. On your terms.”

Evie exhaled slowly, her shoulders loosening for the first time all night. For once, the idea of taking it slow didn’t feel like failure. It felt like freedom.

Canon Arc — The Six-Month Stretch

1. Stepping Back

Evie told Bella and Mandy she needed space, and they respected it. For six months, she poured herself into her teaching and the kids at Strong & Savory. She expanded cooking classes, built new lesson plans, and added small after-school workshops. The joy she found in the children’s laughter reminded her why she started.

2. Invitations Without Pressure

Bella and Mandy didn’t let her disappear. They sent invitations to galas, dinners, and small gatherings — always framed with “only if you want to.” Sometimes Evie declined, sometimes she showed up for an hour, slipping in with her warm smile that made everyone light up. She never felt pushed, only welcomed.

3. Friend Dates with Cole

Cole remained her steady anchor. Every week or two, they slipped away for a “friend date” — movies, late-night diners, long walks, ice cream runs. Nothing serious. No pressure. Just two friends who understood each other’s silences. People whispered, teased, but they both ignored it.

4. The Outfits

Evie slowly began blending the pieces Addie had given her into her everyday life. A soft blouse instead of a t-shirt. A necklace with her teaching clothes. Fitted leggings for a class, though she’d quickly change before cooking to avoid stains. She laughed at herself — “too nice to ruin with flour” — but the shift was undeniable.

5. Personality Glowing Louder

Even in her “normal” clothes, Evie’s personality only grew louder. She laughed more freely, spoke with confidence, teased the kids and parents with warmth that lit up every room. At school auctions, parents began pulling her aside for advice, treating her less like a helper and more like a leader. She didn’t notice it happening — but everyone else did.

6. The Sisters’ Watchful Eye

Bella and Mandy noticed too. Watching her from a distance at events, they whispered with satisfaction. “See? She’s glowing brighter on her own.” They didn’t force her back into gowns or heels — they just kept showing up, making sure she knew she was never alone.

Scene — The Nashville Invitation

Evie was tidying up after a Friday cooking class when Bella and Mandy slipped into the kitchen, each holding an iced coffee like it was contraband.

Mandy hopped onto the counter, grinning. “So. We’ve decided you need a vacation.”

Evie raised an eyebrow. “A vacation?”

Bella leaned against the doorframe, smiling warmly. “A weekend. Nothing fancy. Just Nashville. Good food, good music, and even better company.”

Evie’s hands stilled on the dish towel. “Nashville? Who’s going?”

“Hawk, Matt, me, Mandy,” Bella listed casually. Then she added with a sly smile, “And if you want to bring Cole, it’ll be a great friend getaway. Zero pressure. Just fun.”

Evie blinked, caught off guard. “Wait — me? With you all?”

Mandy smirked. “Yes, you. You’ve been hiding in kid-world for six months. Time to remember you’ve got sisters too. This isn’t gowns and galas. This is boots, barbecue, and late-night live music.”

Bella crossed the room and squeezed Evie’s shoulder. “Think of it as a reset. A chance to laugh, eat too much, sing badly, and just… be with us. No expectations. Just closeness. Just friends.”

Evie hesitated, her heart pounding at the thought of stepping into their orbit again. But the warmth in Bella’s eyes, the mischievous spark in Mandy’s smile, the thought of Cole at her side in a purely safe way — it all tugged at her.

Finally, she smiled, soft but genuine. “Nashville, huh? Alright. Let’s go.”

Mandy raised her coffee in triumph. “Yes! Evie in Nashville. This is going to be fun.”

Bella laughed, her arm still on Evie’s shoulder. “No — this is going to be family.”

Arc — The Nashville Weekend

1. The Invitation to Cole

It was Bella who reached out. She caught Cole at the gym the day after Evie said yes.

“You’re coming to Nashville,” she told him matter-of-factly.

Cole raised an eyebrow. “Am I?”

“Evie’s going,” Bella said, crossing her arms. “She’ll say it’s a group trip — which it is — but she needs her steady anchor there. That’s you. No questions.”

Cole smirked faintly. “So it’s less an invitation and more an order?”

“Exactly,” Bella said, satisfied.



2. The Weekend Begins

The group piled into SUVs and rolled into Nashville on a Friday evening. Hawk found a steakhouse that turned dinner into a feast, and Matt made everyone laugh so hard with his terrible country-song impressions that Evie nearly choked on her sweet tea.

Cole sat beside her, relaxed, his steady presence making the chaos feel safe.



3. The Music & The First Slip

Saturday night, they crowded into a honky-tonk, live music spilling out onto the street. Evie wore one of the low-key outfits Addie had bought her months before — boots, jeans, a soft blouse that made her glow under the neon lights.

Cole leaned close to say something over the music, and when Evie turned, her lips brushed his. A slip. Barely a kiss. But her breath caught, and his eyes lingered on hers longer than they should have before he pulled back, giving her space.

She laughed it off nervously, cheeks pink. “Guess I really can’t dance in crowded bars.”

Cole only smiled, steady as ever. “Guess not.”



4. The Rooftop & The Second Slip

On Sunday evening, after a day of exploring shops and eating far too much barbecue, the group gathered on the rooftop of their hotel with ice cream and drinks. The city glowed beneath them, guitars still faint in the distance.

Evie sat between Bella and Mandy, her laughter bubbling as they teased Matt and Hawk. Then, in a quiet moment, she looked at the two women and blurted without thinking, “You two… you’re the best friends I’ve ever had.”

Bella squeezed her hand instantly, her eyes soft. Mandy leaned her head on Evie’s shoulder with a smug grin. “Took you long enough to admit it.”

Evie laughed, embarrassed but glowing. “I mean it.”



5. Closing the Weekend

When Monday came, they piled back into the cars, tired but smiling. Evie sat in the back seat, gazing out the window, her heart full. She had slipped twice that weekend — once into something she wasn’t ready to name with Cole, and once into the safety of admitting Bella and Mandy were truly her sisters.

For the first time, both slips felt less like mistakes and more like steps forward.
